On physical analysis of topological indices and entropy measures for porphyrazine structure using logarithmic regression model
Authors: Asma Khalid, Shoaib Iqbal, Muhammad Kamran Siddiqui, Tariq Javed Zia, Brima Gegbe
Published: 2024

ABSTRACT
The porphyrazine structure, known for its high chemical and thermal stability, has become a significant focus in materials science, chemical reactivity, functionalization, and drug design. By utilizing the new Zagreb-type indices to analyze the chemical structure of porphyrazine, we can gather more information about their bonding and connecting patterns. This enables us to construct an entropy measure that helps evaluate the stability of the material and predict its behavior in different scenarios. Furthermore, establishing correlations between these indices and entropy using logarithmic regression models allows for a deeper understanding of complex properties of porphyrazine. This, in turn, opens up new possibilities for the compound’s potential applications across various scientific and technical fields. In our work, we have used the M-polynomial to derive molecular descriptors for degree-based topological indices and determine the entropy of the porphyrazine structure based on these descriptors.

Introduction

Graphs, like biological systems, social networks, transportation and communication networks, etc can simulate various systems. The collection of vertices and edges is called a graph that establishes the foundation for more intricate structures. Initially, James Sylvester and Arthur Cayley started applying the mathematical concepts of graph theory to chemistry in 19851. They discovered an isomorphism between the structure of individual chemical compound and the mathematical graphs, demonstrating the relevance of graphs in the chemical context. A connected simple graph is used to represent the molecular structure of chemical compounds, with bonds as edges and atoms as vertices2. This approach helps analyze molecular structures through topological indices, allowing researchers to predict chemical properties without costly lab experiments3. Chemical graph theory offers valuable insights into the structural and topological features of compounds and finds applications in chemical synthesis, material science, drug discovery, and studies like QSPR and QSAR4. The structure of porphyrazine is represented as a simple graph Q, where atoms are represented as vertices (nodes) V(Q), and chemical bonds are represented as edges E(Q). Let Q be a graph, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ represents the degree of the graph Q, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_v$$\end{document}dv denotes the degree of the vertex v5.

Topological indices are numerical values derived from the chemical composition of molecules6. QSPR and QSAR analysis uses these values to compare with the biological and physicochemical properties of the chemical compounds7. The concept of topological descriptors was first introduced by Wiener8 in 1947. The Wiener index has been employed to explain various chemical and physical properties of molecules, as well as to correlate the structure of molecules with their biological activity. The first degree-based topological index was introduced by Gutman et al.9. The Zagreb index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1(Q)$$\end{document}M1(Q) is defined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${d_{u}+d_{v}}$$\end{document}du+dv based on the sum of degrees of the vertices, and these indices are useful for examining how the molecular structure is influenced by the total electron energy. The second Zagreb index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_2(Q)$$\end{document}M2(Q) was introduced in 1975 and defined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${d_{u}d_{v}}$$\end{document}dudv based on the product of degrees of the vertices. These indices help in understanding the sensitivity of a molecular structure and its relationship with the chemical properties of the structure, such as boiling temperature, molecular weight, and surface area. In addition, other similar Zagreb indices have been introduced, including the Augmented Zagreb index AZ(Q) by Furtula et al.10 and the Hyper Zagreb index HM(Q) by Shirdel et al.11, which are defined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \left( \frac{d_{u}d_{v}}{d_{u}+d_{v}-2}\right) ^3$$\end{document}dudvdu+dv-23 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \left( d_{u}+d_{v}\right) ^2$$\end{document}du+dv2, respectively. These indices provide a better understanding of the properties of molecular structure and offer a comprehensive measure of the structural complexity of chemical compounds.

Jianxiu Hao introduced the Modifed Zagreb index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1^m(Q)$$\end{document}M1m(Q) in 201112, defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{{d_{u}d_{v}}}$$\end{document}1dudv depending on the vertex degree’s inverse value. The Modified Zagreb index is used to determine heat formation, chemoinformatics, computational chemistry, and network analysis. M. Randic introduced the general Randic index in 197513, and the other variant of the Randic index is the Harmonic index H(Q), which was introduced by Lingping Zhong in 201214 and is represented by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{2}{{d_{u}+d_{v}}}$$\end{document}2du+dv. The Harmonic index provides valuable insights into the structure and connectivity of graphs and other complex systems, enhancing the analysis and characterization of networks. Gupta et al. introduced the Symmetric division degree index SDD(Q) in 201615. It is a flexible tool used in drug discovery and computational chemistry for the analysis, creation, and prediction of chemical compounds with desired features, defined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d_{u}}{d_{v}}+\frac{d_{v}}{d_{u}}$$\end{document}dudv+dvdu or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{{d_{u}^2+d_{v}^2}}{{d_{u}d_{v}}}$$\end{document}du2+dv2dudv respectively. Furtula et al. introduced the Forgotten index F(Q) in 201516, which is defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{u}^2+d_{v}^2$$\end{document}du2+dv2, and these are used to analyze and characterize networks in chemoinformatics, network research, and computational biology. Sedlar et al. introduced the inverse sum index I(Q) in 201517, defined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{{d_{u}d_{v}}}{{d_{u}+d_{v}}}$$\end{document}dudvdu+dv. This index is valuable for comparing structural complexity in molecular isomers and finding correlations between molecular structure and properties in areas like drug design and material science, where it influences properties such as stability, reactivity, and binding affinities. Amer et al. introduced the sigma index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma (Q)$$\end{document}σ(Q) in 1900, which is defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(d_{u}-d_{v})^2$$\end{document}(du-dv)2.

A lot of work is being done on topological indices, and some algebraists are also trying to make good use of graphs by using the physicochemical properties of topological indices. The Hosoya polynomial, introduced by Hosoya in 1988, plays a key role in distance-based topological indices18. In 2015, Deutsch and Klavžar19 introduced the M-polynomial, one of two important new tools in this area. Later, in 2021, Mondal et al.20 proposed the NM-polynomial, which is essential for analyzing degree-based topological indices. The M-polynomial has proven to be highly effective in deriving topological indices, and the rapid advancement in this field has led to the frequent introduction of new indices. Recent research has focused on neighborhood degree sum-based indices, particularly exploring NM-polynomials in the context of graphene structures.

The NM-polynomial encodes molecular structures with high precision, remains invariant under graph isomorphisms, and offers computational advantages, making it a powerful tool for studying chemical graphs and providing deeper insights than simpler indices21,22. The M-polynomial plays a significant role in graph theory, particularly in the analysis of chemical graphs and molecular structures. It enables the efficient computation of different topological indices, which are important for comprehending the chemical and structural characteristics of compounds. Additionally, the M-polynomial remains unchanged under graph isomorphisms, making it a valuable tool for comparing and distinguishing molecular structures. It also allows for the calculation of molecular descriptors used in QSAR and QSPR studies, which are crucial for drug discovery and materials science23. Recent studies on porphyrazine graphs focus on exploring their potential applications in molecular structure, catalysis, drug delivery, and photodynamic therapy due to their unique chemical properties24. In25 Ozturk Sozen et al. studied some drugs used in the treatment of breast cancer and they calculated topological coindices by using the algebraic approach. Also in Ref.26 Ozturk Sozen et al. explore the computation of topological descriptors for prime ideal sum graphs of commutative rings. Nadeem et al.27 computed the topological aspects of metal–organic structures. Ahmad et al.28,29 discuss the theoretical study of energy of phenylene and anthracene. Koam et al.30 determined the valency-based topological descriptor for Hexagon Star Networks.

Computational techniques of M-polynomial

Let’s suppose the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {V}=\left\{ (\mathfrak {y},\mathfrak {z})\in \mathbb {N} \times \mathbb {N} \vert \ 1\leqslant \mathfrak {y}\leqslant \mathfrak {z}\leqslant \kappa \right\} $$\end{document}V=(y,z)∈N×N|1⩽y⩽z⩽κ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{u v}=|\left\{ u v \in E(Q)|\ d_{u}=\mathfrak {y},\ d_{v}=\mathfrak {z}\right\} |$$\end{document}duv=|uv∈E(Q)|du=y,dv=z|. According to Deutsch et al.19, the M-polynomial for a connected simple graph Q is defined as:1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M(Q,r,s)= \displaystyle \sum _{\mathfrak {y} \leqslant \mathfrak {z}} \mathfrak {E}_{\mathfrak {y}\mathfrak {z}}r^{\mathfrak {y}}s^{\mathfrak {z}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M(Q,r,s)=∑y⩽zEyzrysz,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathfrak {E}_{\mathfrak {y}\mathfrak {z}}$$\end{document}Eyz be the total number of edges \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$uv \in E(Q)$$\end{document}uv∈E(Q) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lbrace d_u,d_v\rbrace = \lbrace \mathfrak {y},\mathfrak {z}\rbrace $$\end{document}{du,dv}={y,z}. Derivation form of some vertex degree based M-polynomial molecular descriptors.

Graph index-entropy

In 1948, Shannon first introduced the concept of entropy in his influential study analyzing data from graphs and their chemical structures. Its application has been widely expanded in the field of chemical networks and graph structures. Based on the grouping of vertex orbits, Rashevsky31 first introduced graph entropy in 1955. Graph entropies are commonly utilized across multiple disciplines, including sociology, biology, ecology, chemistry, etc. Different types of chemical graphs are analyzed using graph entropy. The graph’s vertices, edges, and other parts are related to their probability distributions according to the graph’s intrinsic and intrinsic entropy metrics32. The definition of entropy for a connected graph Q was given by Dehmer33, which assesses and gathers data’s structural properties. Let Q be a connected graph, entropy of graph Q is defined as:2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {H}_{\mathfrak {f}}}(Q)=-\displaystyle \sum _{\mathfrak {i}=1}^{\mathfrak {m}} \frac{\mathfrak {f}(e_i)}{ \displaystyle \sum \nolimits _{\mathfrak {i}=1}^{\mathfrak {m}} \mathfrak {f}(e_{\mathfrak {i}})} \log \Biggl ( \frac{\mathfrak {f}(e_{\mathfrak {i}})}{\displaystyle \sum \nolimits _{\mathfrak {i}=1}^{\mathfrak {m}} \mathfrak {f}(e_{\mathfrak {i}})}\Biggl ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hf(Q)=-∑i=1mf(ei)∑i=1mf(ei)log(f(ei)∑i=1mf(ei)),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}$$\end{document}m represents the rank of Q, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {f}$$\end{document}f is an arbitrary information function. Main result

There are two parts to this section. First, we use the degree sum edge partition strategy to compute the M-polynomial of the porphyrazine structure and provide numerical and graphical representations in “M-polynomial of porphyrazine graph” section. Second, we investigate the index entropies of the porphyrazine structure concerning the M-polynomial in “Index-entropies of porphyrazine graph” section, and provide corresponding graphical and numerical visualizations.

M-polynomial of porphyrazine graph

Porphyrazine’s structure is made up of azomethine bridges or meso-nitrogen atoms, which link its aromatic rings instead of the methine bridges seen in porphyrins. There are two main methods to change the physical and chemical properties of these macrocycles. One method involves substituting the metal cations at the core, while the other involves altering the outer structure with different substituents. Because of its distinct electrochemical and optical properties, porphyrazine has important uses in healthcare and technology. The three-dimensional structure of porphyrazine is shown on the left side of Fig. 1, providing a clearer view of its molecular bonding.The two-dimensional unit structure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n=1)$$\end{document}(n=1), with 34 vertices and 41 edges, is shown on the right side. In Fig. 1, and the gray dots show carbon atoms the blue dots stand for nitrogen atoms, the red dots represent hydrogen atoms. A more comprehensive depiction of the structure is provided by Fig. 2, which additionally displays two \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n=2)$$\end{document}(n=2) porphyrazine units connected together, with 66 vertices and 82 edges.Fig. 1Porphyrazine’s unit molecular structure (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=1$$\end{document}n=1).Fig. 2Porphyrazine’s double molecular structure (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=2$$\end{document}n=2).

Porphyrazine’s unit molecular structure (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=1$$\end{document}n=1).

Degree-based edge partition of porphyrazine graph.

The vertex partitioning of porphyrazine graph is given in Table 2, and degree based edge partition is givin in Table 3. In Theorem 1 we find the M-polynomial of the porphyrazine structure and after that we have our main results.

Theorem 1

If Q represents porphyrazine structure, then M-polynomial is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}M(Q,r,s)=(4n+4)rs^3+nrs^5+(12n-4)r^2s^3+20nr^3s^3+4nr^3s^5.\end{aligned}$$\end{document}M(Q,r,s)=(4n+4)rs3+nrs5+(12n-4)r2s3+20nr3s3+4nr3s5.

Proof

The unit graph of porphyrazine structure has 34 vertices and 41 edges, based on the vertex degree, porphyrazine structure is divided into edge sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {V}=\left\{ (1,3),(1,5),(2,3),(3,3),(3,5) \right\} $$\end{document}V=(1,3),(1,5),(2,3),(3,3),(3,5) and the edge partition of porphyrazine structure is given in Table 3 and also the edge set is divided into five parts:

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{13}=\left\{ u v \in E(Q)| \ d_{u}=1, \ d_{v}=3 \right\} $$\end{document}d13=uv∈E(Q)|du=1,dv=3,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{15}=\left\{ u v \in E(Q)| \ d_{u}=1, \ d_{v}=5 \right\} $$\end{document}d15=uv∈E(Q)|du=1,dv=5,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{23}=\left\{ u v \in E(Q)| \ d_{u}=2, \ d_{v}=3 \right\} $$\end{document}d23=uv∈E(Q)|du=2,dv=3,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{33}=\left\{ u v \in E(Q)| \ d_{u}=3, \ d_{v}=3 \right\} $$\end{document}d33=uv∈E(Q)|du=3,dv=3,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{35}=\left\{ u v \in E(Q)| \ d_{u}=3, \ d_{v}= 5\right\} $$\end{document}d35=uv∈E(Q)|du=3,dv=5,

such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\displaystyle \sum _{1 \leqslant 3} \mathfrak {E}_{13}=4n+4, \ \ \ \displaystyle \sum _{1 \leqslant 5} \mathfrak {E}_{15}=n, \ \ \ \displaystyle \sum _{2 \leqslant 3} \mathfrak {E}_{23}=12n-4,\ \ \ \displaystyle \sum _{3 \leqslant 3} \mathfrak {E}_{33}=20n,\ \ \ \displaystyle \sum _{3 \leqslant 5} \mathfrak {E}_{35}=n.\end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑1⩽3E13=4n+4,∑1⩽5E15=n,∑2⩽3E23=12n-4,∑3⩽3E33=20n,∑3⩽5E35=n.By Eq. (1), M-polynomial of porphyrazine structure is defined as,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}M(Q,r,s)= \displaystyle \sum _{1 \leqslant 3} \mathfrak {E}_{13}rs^3+\displaystyle \sum _{1 \leqslant 5} \mathfrak {E}_{15}rs^5+\displaystyle \sum _{2 \leqslant 3} \mathfrak {E}_{23}r^2s^3+\displaystyle \sum _{3 \leqslant 3} \mathfrak {E}_{33}r^3s^{3}+\displaystyle \sum _{3 \leqslant 5} \mathfrak {E}_{35}r^3s^5\end{aligned}$$\end{document}M(Q,r,s)=∑1⩽3E13rs3+∑1⩽5E15rs5+∑2⩽3E23r2s3+∑3⩽3E33r3s3+∑3⩽5E35r3s5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}=(4n+4)rs^3+nrs^5+(12n-4)r^2s^3+20nr^3s^3+4nr^3s^5. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}=(4n+4)rs3+nrs5+(12n-4)r2s3+20nr3s3+4nr3s5.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Now, we have used this M-polynomial to calculate some molecular descriptors of porphyrazine structure in next Theorem.

Graphical representation of porphyrazine via M-polynomial indices.

We observed that the numerical values of AZ(Q) and I(Q) increase more rapidly compared to HM(Q) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_2(Q)$$\end{document}M2(Q). Logarithmic regression and its analysis

Regression analysis is a statistical method used to model the relationship between a dependent variable and one or more independent variables34,35. This technique is widely applied in fields such as economics, finance, social sciences, and engineering to understand how independent factors influence the dependent variable and to generate predictive estimates36. The primary goal of regression analysis is to identify the best-fitting line or curve that represents the relationship between the variables. In some models, the natural logarithm of the dependent variable is used to represent entropy in relation to the indices of the independent variable. The dependent variable, also known as the response or outcome variable, is the one being predicted or explained, while the independent variables are those that are hypothesized to influence it37. For these studies, we used SPSS software (https://www.ibm.com/products/spss-statistics) to conduct the analysis. Among various types of regression, simple linear regression is most commonly used, involving a single independent variable and assuming a linear relationship between the variables38. The line’s equation can be written as,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}Y=\alpha _0+\alpha _1X_1+\alpha _2X_2+\alpha _3X_3+\cdots +\alpha _nX_n+\epsilon ,\end{aligned}$$\end{document}Y=α0+α1X1+α2X2+α3X3+⋯+αnXn+ϵ,where the Y-intercept is indicated by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _0$$\end{document}α0, the independent variable’s coefficient are represented by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_m$$\end{document}Xm where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m = 1,2,3,...,n$$\end{document}m=1,2,3,...,n, the molecular descriptor (independent variable) is X, and the error value is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon $$\end{document}ϵ.

Regression analysis is used to estimate the coefficients \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _0$$\end{document}α0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _i$$\end{document}αi by minimizing the sum of squared differences between the actual values of Y and the values predicted by the model. The least squares method is commonly employed to obtain these estimates. Additionally, regression analysis provides several statistical metrics to evaluate the model’s validity, including the coefficient of determination \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document}R2, which measures the proportion of variation in the dependent variable explained by the independent variables. This technique is particularly useful for identifying correlations between variables, making predictions, and exploring cause-and-effect relationships. Key applications of regression analysis include forecasting, time series modeling, and examining causal connections between variables39.

Logarithmic regression analysis, also known as logarithmic transformation or log-linear regression40, is a statistical technique used to model the relationship between a dependent variable and one or more independent variables. It is particularly useful when the nature of the relationship is better represented on a logarithmic scale.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}Y=\alpha _0+\alpha _1 \log X_1+\alpha _2 \log X_2+\alpha _3 \log X_3+\cdots +\alpha _n \log X_n+\epsilon .\end{aligned}$$\end{document}Y=α0+α1logX1+α2logX2+α3logX3+⋯+αnlogXn+ϵ.

In this model, the dependent variable (entropy) is represented by Y, the independent variable (molecular descriptors) is X, the logarithmic function is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\log $$\end{document}log, the error term is denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon $$\end{document}ϵ, the Y-intercept by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _0$$\end{document}α0, and the coefficients of the molecular descriptors for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i = 1,2,3,...,n$$\end{document}i=1,2,3,...,n by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _i$$\end{document}αi. Logarithmic transformation is useful for aligning the data and making extreme values more manageable by reducing imbalances. It is often applied when variables have a curvilinear relationship with diminishing returns or increasing rates of change41. Logarithmic regression has broad applications across various fields, including biology, environmental sciences, finance, and economics42. The logarithmic scale enables researchers to explore and record non-linear relationships in the data, as well as to predict or infer complex associations.

Discussion on computational results

We used SPSS software to explore the relationship between molecular descriptors and graph entropy by applying power and logarithmic regression models. The logarithmic model shows a closer fit to the data, as its curve nearly touches every data point, indicating a stronger fit compared to the power model. Consequently, logarithmic regression was preferred for investigating the relationship between molecular descriptors and graph entropy due to its superior fit. Regression analysis was used to identify the best predictors among the variables, with the strongest relationships indicating the most effective predictors. In this case, the variables exhibited curvilinear relationships, making logarithmic regression the most appropriate model. Our analysis revealed that the connection between AZ(Q) and the corresponding entropy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_{\mathfrak {f}_4}(Q)$$\end{document}Hf4(Q), is significantly stronger than that of other molecular descriptors, as the logarithmic model’s curve consistently aligned with each point of AZ(Q).

R is the correlation coefficient that measures the strength and direction of the linear relationship between the independent and dependent variables. Its value ranges from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-1$$\end{document}-1 to 1, where values close to 1 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-1$$\end{document}-1 indicate a strong positive or negative linear relationship, respectively, and values near 0 suggest little to no linear relationship. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document}R2 represents the proportion of the variance in the dependent variable that is explained by the independent variables in the regression model. Its values range from 0 to 1, with higher values indicating that the model explains a greater portion of the variability in the data. The F-value is a ratio that compares the explained variance to the unexplained variance (residuals) in the regression model. It assesses the overall significance of the model and tests whether the independent variables collectively have a statistically significant effect on the dependent variable. A higher F-value typically indicates that the model is statistically significant. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_e$$\end{document}Se measures the average distance that the observed values fall from the regression line. It provides an estimate of the accuracy of the predicted values and the regression coefficients and a lower standard error indicates more precise estimates and a better-fitting model. Here, the indices and entropy in the Figs. 5, 6, 7, 8 and 9 are represented by distinct symbols \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Mm1(G)=M_1^m(Q)$$\end{document}Mm1(G)=M1m(Q), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} When the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document}R2 value is 1, it indicates a perfect fit, meaning that the changes in entropy measures are fully explained by the logarithmic regression models. Based on the high \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document}R2 values, it is hypothesized that the natural logarithms of the topological indices (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1(Q)$$\end{document}M1(Q), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_2(Q)$$\end{document}M2(Q), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1^m(Q)$$\end{document}M1m(Q), AZ(Q), H(Q), HM(Q), F(Q), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma (Q)$$\end{document}σ(Q), SDD(Q), and I(Q)) and their respective entropy measures \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_{\mathfrak {f}_1}(Q)$$\end{document}Hf1(Q) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_{\mathfrak {f}_{10}}(Q)$$\end{document}Hf10(Q) exhibit a very strong and linear connection. These entropy changes closely reflect changes in various molecular descriptors. The positive coefficients in all equations indicate positive correlations between entropy measures and the natural logarithm of the indices. The best relationship was found between AZ(Q) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_{\mathfrak {f}_{4}}(Q)$$\end{document}Hf4(Q), with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R = 1$$\end{document}R=1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2 = 1$$\end{document}R2=1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{S}{e} = 0.000$$\end{document}Se=0.000, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{F} = 2.611 \times 10^{14}$$\end{document}F=2.611×1014. This model has the highest values of R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document}R2, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{F}$$\end{document}F, along with the lowest \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{S}_{e}$$\end{document}Se, making AZ(Q) the most accurate predictor of the complexity of the porphyrazine structure. In QSPR modeling, as reported in Ref.43, the first Zagreb index in a cubic regression model is the best predictor for molar refractivity, while the augmented Zagreb index is the most effective in linear models for polar surface area in quadratic regression. The harmonic index is the best predictor for both quadratic and cubic regression models of molar volume. Meanwhile, the forgotten index is highly correlated with boiling point. Additionally, there is a strong predictive relationship between polarizability and molar refractivity and the second modified Zagreb index. In QSPR and QSAR analysis, the second modified Zagreb index has a strong predictive correlation with molar refractivity and polarizability, as demonstrated in Ref.44.Table 8Numerical values for Tables 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, and 13 shows the relationship between different M-polynomial topological indices and its entropies by using logarithmic regression model.

Figures 5, 6, 7, 8, and 9 shows the graphical visualization of logarithmic regression models.

Conclusion

The M-polynomial equation for the porphyrazine structure is thoroughly derived and discussed in this study. Topological indices are numerical values that describe the topological features of chemical compounds, such as their shape, connectivity, and symmetry. These indices can be used to predict the biological activity or physicochemical properties of chemical compounds, including anticancer activity. Topological indices have been utilized in cancer drug development to create QSAR models that predict anticancer efficacy based on drug candidates’ molecular structures. These models can help identify promising drug candidates for further experimental testing and optimization. In the context of cancer drug discovery, QSAR and QSPR models can be used to predict the anticancer activity or other relevant properties of drug candidates. These models are typically built using machine learning algorithms and require a large data set of chemical structures and their corresponding biological activities or properties.

SPSS software is used to study the correlation between molecular descriptors and graph entropy with the help of logarithmic and power regression. The logarithmic regression model curve almost touches every point in the observed data set, indicating that its line is closer than the power regression model. As a result, we conclude that the logarithmic model outperforms the power model. We can also conclude that the relationship between AZ(Q) and its related entropy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_{\mathfrak {f}_4}(Q)$$\end{document}Hf4(Q) shows a significantly stronger relationship than that of other molecular descriptors, as the logarithmic model’s curve goes through each point of AZ(Q) perfectly. Since the logarithmic model’s curve crosses over every point in AZ(Q), we may conclude that the relationship between AZ(Q) and the entropy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} Thus, we can conclude that the most accurate predictor of the complexity of porphyrazine structure is AZ(Q). M-polynomial-based topological indices have higher predictive accuracy for molar volume, molar refractivity, and polarizability, as shown in the QSPR study discussed in Ref.44. The augmented Zagreb index best predicts polar surface area in the linear models and quadratic regression models, so our computing results help in finding polar surface area, molar volume, molar refractivity, and polarizability for the porphyrazine structure.
